TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

  Foreign Policy | 10 Nov 00:17 UTC

  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. It should raise even greater concern about how Jordan’s government has used billions in U.S. security assistance and more than a decade of support for countering violent extremism. The main perpetrator of the attack had only recently left prison following a terrorism conviction, yet the government has offered little explanation of how he went on to carry out the massacre. Instead, it launched kneejerk airstrikes on alleged Islamic State hideouts in Syria, killing seven civilians, three of them children, according to Syrian authorities. The House now has until Wednesday to schedule a vote. The chances of the articles passing are “far more likely now than before the election,” Massie told CNN on Monday. Impeaching Hegseth requires a simple majority vote in the House. * Hegseth’s Leadership Has Damaged the Pentagon
  Foreign Policy | 09 Nov 21:32 UTC

  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. On Monday, U.S. Rep. Thomas Massie introduced articles of impeachment against Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, triggering a vote in the House of Representatives that could come as early as this week. Massie, a Republican who will leave Congress in January, accuses Hegseth of waging an “unlawful” war in Iran and abusing the office. Hegseth will be the first civilian leader of the U.S. military establishment to face impeachment in 150 years. The last, William Belknap, was President Ulysses S. Grant’s secretary of war—the title at the time for the civilian leader of the U.S. Army, before a unified Defense Department. The House voted unanimously to impeach him. Belknap resigned ahead of his Senate trial; he avoided conviction because of many senators’ discomfort with impeaching a private citizen. (Impeachments do not require the accused to be in office.) * It’s Possible to Fix the United Nations
  Foreign Policy | 09 Nov 20:49 UTC
  How to break the deadlock on U.N. Security Council reform.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. International order is not the absence of conflict, nor is it the rigid defense of the status quo. Rather, it is the existence of predictable mechanisms to manage inevitable disagreements. And the primary mechanism tasked with upholding international peace and security—the United Nations Security Council (UNSC)—is no longer fit for purpose . The UNSC still resembles the world of 1945, giving privileged status to the victors of World War II and the holders of colonial empires. Without reform to give rising and middle powers a status commensurate with their growing influence, states will eventually see more sense in pursuing their security interests unilaterally or through exclusive, destabilizing blocs . For now, with a few notable exceptions, states remain committed to speaking the language of multilateralism. But make no mistake: Reforming the UNSC is not a matter of aesthetics or desirability; it is an absolute necessity. International order is not the absence of conflict, nor is it the rigid defense of the status quo. Rather, it is the existence of predictable mechanisms to manage inevitable disagreements. And the primary mechanism tasked with upholding international peace and security—the United Nations Security Council (UNSC)—is no longer fit for purpose . The UNSC still resembles the world of 1945, giving privileged status to the victors of World War II and the holders of colonial empires. Without reform to give rising and middle powers a status commensurate with their growing influence, states will eventually see more sense in pursuing their security interests unilaterally or through exclusive, destabilizing blocs . For now, with a few notable exceptions, states remain committed to speaking the language of multilateralism. But make no mistake: Reforming the UNSC is not a matter of aesthetics or desirability; it is an absolute necessity. Three dynamics have held up reform. But each may be slowly giving way, creating a rare window for genuine change. The first and most commonly cited obstacle is the Security Council’s five permanent veto-wielding members, or P5—the United States, China, Russia, France, and the United Kingdom. The P5 countries are wary of admitting rising powers as fellow veto wielders, as it would diminish their own influence in the body and make it more susceptible to deadlock .   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/09/united-nations-security-council-reform-fix/

As the haggling gets underway, let’s take a minute to lay out the facts—and sidestep the vitriol and virtue signaling that tends to dominate social media when it comes to Israel. Based on the final results of the election held two weeks ago, Netanyahu and his bloc have 53 seats in the 120-seat parliament, while Eisenkot’s change bloc controls 48 seats. The remaining 19 seats belong to two Arab-led parties, the Joint List and Ra’am. The nod from the president means that Eisenkot gets the first crack at forming a government. The clearest path to a majority would be for him to form a coalition with the Joint List and Ra’am. That would give the anti-Netanyahu forces a commanding 67-seat majority in Israel’s parliament—enough to address some of the most divisive issues in Israeli society, including expanding mandatory military service to include ultra-Orthodox Jews and establishing a state commission of inquiry into the security failures that led to Hamas’s attack on Oct. 7, 2023. But things are never so straightforward, especially in Israeli politics. * Analysis: Fossil-fuel CO2 emissions set to dip in 2026 as Iran conflict hits oil use
  Carbon Brief | 10 Nov 01:01 UTC
  Carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions from fossil fuels and cement are projected to fall by around 0.4% in 2026 to 38.2bn… The post Analysis: Fossil-fuel CO2 emissions set to dip in 2026 as Iran conflict hits oil use appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions from fossil fuels and cement are projected to fall by around 0.4% in 2026 to 38.2bn tonnes of CO2 (GtCO2), according to new figures from the Global Carbon Project . Carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions from fossil fuels and cement are projected to fall by around 0.4% in 2026 to 38.2bn tonnes of CO2 (GtCO2), according to new figures from the Global Carbon Project . The 21st edition of the annual “global carbon budget” report, published today , says that this small decline is largely due to a 1.9% drop in emissions from oil and the energy shock triggered by the Iran conflict and the closure of the strait of Hormuz. Emissions from coal continue to increase slightly. The Global Carbon Project’s projections for fossil fuel and cement CO2 emissions in 2026 are preliminary, with a range that spans a fall of -1.5% to a rise of +0.6%. This means a small increase in emissions can not be ruled out. Emissions from land-use change , such as deforestation, are projected to rise in 2026. This is primarily driven by peat fires and forest degradation in south-east Asia and is linked to the development of a strong El Niño event this year. As a result, total CO2 emissions from fossil fuels and land use are projected to reach a record high of 43.3GtCO2 in 2026 – marginally above 42.8GtCO2 in 2025. Research published alongside the report by the same team in Nature Climate Change finds that the growth in solar, wind and electric vehicles (EVs) now offsets around two-thirds of the emissions that would otherwise come from rising energy demand. However, the deployment of these technologies is not yet enough to push global emissions into a sustained decline, according to the study. The 2026 global carbon budget report also estimates that: (For more on previous editions of the Global Carbon Project’s annual report, see Carbon Brief’s coverage in 2025 , 2024 , 2023 and 2022 .) Total global CO2 emissions, which combines fossil fuels and land-use change, are projected to reach a record high of 43.3GtCO2 in 2026, up from 42.8GtCO2 in 2025. The increase in 2026 comes entirely from a projected rise in land-use emissions, which are more challenging to estimate than fossil emissions. (The global carbon budget groups emissions from fossil fuels and cement production as “fossil emissions”. Carbon Brief follows this convention throughout this piece.) If land-use emissions had stayed at 2025 levels, total emissions would have fallen slightly in 2026 to around 42.6GtCO2.
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/analysis-fossil-fuel-co2-emissions-set-to-dip-in-2026-as-iran-conflict-hits-oil-use

* Q&A: What is the Belém-Antalya mechanism for a ‘just transition’?
  Carbon Brief | 09 Nov 08:00 UTC
  One of the key expected outcomes from the upcoming COP31 climate summit in Antalya, Turkey, is the launch of a… The post Q&A: What is the Belém-Antalya mechanism for a ‘just transition’? appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: One of the key expected outcomes from the upcoming COP31 climate summit in Antalya, Turkey, is the launch of a “just transition mechanism”. This follows years of calls from civil society to support a “ just transition ” as countries shift towards low-emissions energy systems and away from fossil fuels. At the COP30 UN climate talks last year in the Brazilian city of Belém parties agreed to establish a “mechanism” to support this goal, called the “Belém-Antalya mechanism” (BAM). Groups that have championed the BAM want it to act as a central hub on just transitions, providing support for workers and communities around the world. However, the details of exactly what the much-touted BAM will consist of remain vague, with developed and developing countries disagreeing about its functions and purpose. Disagreements continued at the UN climate talks in Bonn in June 2026, including on the crucial question of how the BAM will be financed. Here, Carbon Brief looks at where the idea for the BAM came from, how it could support just transitions and what to expect from COP31. A “ just transition ” is the idea that workers and communities should be supported in the shift to a low-carbon economy. It is a central principle behind the idea of climate justice . Often, the term refers to those linked to the fossil-fuel industry, but it can also refer to transitions taking place in agriculture , heavy industry and various other sectors. The concept originated in the 1980s, when US labour unions forged alliances with environmental justice groups. It has since become a key part of international climate discussions. The preamble to the 2015 Paris Agreement includes a commitment to “tak[e] into account the imperatives of a just transition of the workforce and the creation of decent work and quality jobs in accordance with nationally defined development priorities”. Bert De Wel , policy officer at the International Trade Union Confederation (ITUC), tells Carbon Brief: “The labour movement is very happy with the wide audience and broad political support that the concept has received since its inception in the 1980s. The inclusion in the Paris Agreement, with the reference to the creation of decent work and quality jobs was a milestone for the movement. While other demands have been added…we continue to stress the labour dimension as being central to the concept.” Three years after the creation of the Paris Agreement, at COP24 in the former coal-mining city of Katowice, the Polish p
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/qa-what-is-the-belem-antalya-mechanism-for-a-just-transition

* Maintenance Futures
  Longreads | 09 Nov 18:48 UTC
  "Future visions tend to organize around narratives of gain or loss, dystopia or utopia. But what of the neutral position?"
  FULL TEXT: On his website, designer Christopher Butler envisions the future. As a culture, we typically default to two versions: one of less, or dystopia; and one of more, or utopia. What a limited way of thinking, no? The opposites can be the case. “A future of less can be a utopia. Not comfortably, but the case is available. The American dream is not a life that ten billion people can hold at once; the square footage, the car, the wealth per household, the arithmetic has never worked,” he writes. And then he goes on: “A future of more can also be a dystopia. I would argue that this is the corner we presently live in.” His contemplative essay explores limits, growth, creativity, and what he refers to as maintenance futurism. Which means the idea rests on a distinction that’s easy to state and hard to keep: maintenance chosen is a practice, and maintenance imposed is a lid. Our basement storehouse is a decision we made about our present and future. It would be a different space entirely if someone had measured it out for us. “How techno’s most vaunted architect is still building sonic futures.” “It’s been an idea for over three decades. How did the clock that will run for 10,000 years become a reality?” “If you had to store something for 100 years, how would you do it?” “Silicon Valley has always dreamed of building its own utopias. Who’s ready to move in?” “De-extinction promises not a true reversal of time but a selective one.” “A burgeoning, surrealist art hub on the shores of a toxic lake hints at how disaster can foster new degrees of care.” Cheri has been an editor at Longreads since 2014. When Rahul noticed that her shoelace had come undone, he bent down to tie it himself, creating a tender moment that quickly went viral. The march helped to retire Rahul’s image as a part-time politician who appeared before elections and vanished afterward, replacing it with that of a man willing to spend months on the road, listening before speaking. Rather than resorting to difficult-to-follow jargon in news interviews, he spoke of opening a “mohabbat ki dukaan” (“shop of love”) in a “nafrat ka bazaar” (“marketplace of hate”). The post Netanyahu’s ‘Poison Machine’ Could Turn His Loss Into a Win appeared first on New L...
  FULL TEXT: In most countries, if a prime minister were to lose his parliamentary majority in a national election, one would conclude that he was out of power. Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu saw his governing coalition decline from 68 to 53 seats after the Oct. 27 national election, so the natural assumption might be that he had suffered a major upset and was going to be replaced. This, however, is not what happened in Israel after the ballots were counted last week. Netanyahu, who has been in power for 19 years partly because he is a master of the political game, lost; but he also, somehow, won. The bloc of parties that oppose Netanyahu is composed of two smaller blocs. The larger one includes four parties that identify as Zionist, and the largest of these is Gadi Eisenkot’s Yashar party; collectively, they won 48 seats. The smaller sub-bloc is composed of two groupings of mostly Palestinian parties that in Israel are referred to as the “Arab parties,” although one of them, the binational Democratic Front for Peace and Equality, identifies as an Arab-Jewish party. Importantly for the Jewish majority, these parties do not identify as Zionist. Collectively, thanks to the Arab community’s burning need to end the rule of Israel’s right-wing government and particularly of its minister of internal security, Itamar Ben-Gvir, they won an unprecedented 19 seats. Together, the Jewish-Zionist and non-Zionist Arab parties have a comfortable 67-seat majority, more than enough to form a government. A government with the Arab parties, which together won 19 seats, or even a minority government kept alive by their votes from outside the coalition, has been taboo in Israeli politics for decades. The right’s campaign to delegitimize Arab participation in government has thus achieved what Netanyahu could not achieve at the ballot box, which is to help keep Netanyahu in power. Unless the Jewish-Zionist opposition parties accept a government that relies on Arab votes, Netanyahu stays in power — either at the head of a new majority, if one of the parties in the opposition breaks its vow and joins him (which would sink the political career of the party leader, as occurred with Benny Gantz), or as caretaker prime minister until an early election in 2027. For decades, neither Jewish nor Arab parties sought Arab participation in government. * How the Houthis’ Enemies Made Them a Regional Power
  New Lines | 09 Nov 11:09 UTC
  The Houthis owe their rise less to Tehran than to their foes, from former President Saleh’s shifting allegiances and the Saudi-Emirati rift to a Western response that has normalized the Red Sea threat. The post How the Houthis’ Enemies Made Them a Regional Power appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Twenty-two years since their emergence as a rebel group in Yemen, the Houthis have become arguably the most powerful nonstate actor in the world. How and why did they manage such a feat? There is no doubt that Iran was significant in empowering and strengthening the movement and its military capabilities. Yet Iranian support, and the Yemeni army arsenal they seized, are not the only factors behind the Houthis’ strength. Their enemies’ strategic mistakes, miscalculations, and absence of coherent strategies and policies — along with incompetence, inefficiency and divisions among local rivals — also boosted and emboldened them. There was a strategic myopia among all actors — local, regional and international. The Houthis are often described as lucky to have had such irrational and fragmented enemies. Even Yemen’s rugged terrain worked to their advantage. And in the end, they emerged as such a powerful force that their ambitions are no longer confined by geographic boundaries. In 2004, a group of fighters in the mountainous terrain of Saada governorate, bordering Saudi Arabia, began challenging the authority of the government of former President Ali Abdullah Saleh. The group gradually transformed from al-Shabab al-Mumin (“The Faithful Youth”), as they were first known in the 1990s, into Ansar Allah, better known as the Houthi movement, led by Hussein al-Houthi. They began chanting slogans similar to those of the Islamic Republic of Iran, such as “Death to America” and “Death to Israel.” Their behavior angered Saleh. At that time, few were even aware of the Houthis’ existence, though in small numbers they were chanting these slogans as early as 2002, according to some reports. Hussein al-Houthi was a member of Parliament at the time. Saleh considered him and his supporters a challenge to his authority in Saada. Mediation efforts aimed at convincing them to hand themselves over to the police were unsuccessful, and clashes erupted on June 18, 2004, as government forces attempted to arrest the Houthi leader. Mediation continued even after the clashes, but al-Houthi refused to surrender. On Sept. 10, the government announced the killing of the rebel leader and the end of the insurgency. Yet the latter would survive, even outlasting Saleh’s regime. After Hussein al-Houthi was killed, his father Badreddine took over for several months before being succeeded by Abdul-Malik, Hussein’s elder brother and the current leader. * Equality talk
  Aeon | 09 Nov 11:00 UTC
  In the summer of 1646, the idea of equality gained new and profound depths. We are still catching up with the consequences - by Teresa M Bejan Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Striking dockworkers, England, 1954. Photo by Marc Riboud/Magnum Photos It’s no accident that equality comes first among the ‘self-evident’ truths articulated in the United States Declaration of Independence. The claim that ‘all men are created equal’ serves there not simply as an abstract premise from which revolutionary consequences ought to follow, but as a concrete practical foundation on which the American colonists could claim to stand as equals with the greatest king and hold him to account. Still impressed by his own handiwork 50 years later, Thomas Jefferson even prophesied that the ‘palpable truth’ of equality enshrined in the Declaration would one day unite all of humankind. Yet today, it’s evident that the word ‘equality’ means very different things to different people. What once seemed capable of providing common ground is now just another source of controversy. What explains this shift? I suspect that the deceptively simple, conceptual language of ‘equality’ has itself played a substantial role. A better understanding of ‘equality talk’ – as the application of this language to human beings and their relations – might therefore shed some light on our predicament. L et’s begin by examining a group of people that generally prides itself on thinking (and speaking) more clearly than most: professional philosophers. What are they doing when they talk about equality? Often, they’re saying something about human beings: about what we’re fundamentally like, and how we should be treated morally, politically and socially. When an academic philosopher says that ‘all people are equal’, she’s generally saying that, despite differences of race and gender, economic class or individual merit, every person is somehow the same in some fundamental respect(s). While some philosophers will stress the human capacity for reason and moral agency, others emphasise our mutual vulnerability to suffering and violent death. Still, all generally agree that this sameness – whatever its underlying basis – ought to matter for our social and political arrangements, such that any persistent inequalities between human beings must require some justification or redress. But here, a non-philosopher might wonder why the professionals insist on using the technical language of ‘equality’ as opposed to something more straightforward like ‘sameness’? One reason, I think, is that they are trading on the former concept’s mathematical resonances of quantitative precision and transcendent tr
  https://aeon.co/essays/historical-ideas-of-equality-help-us-imagine-an-equal-world

  The reactions in Egypt to Egyptian-American Abdul El-Sayed’s U.S. Senate victory ranged from exuberant celebration and online humor to indifference and outright rejection, exposing the limits of Egypt’s embrace of its own abroad. The post Egypt Reacts to Abdul El-Sayed’s Win appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: (Photo by: Jeff Kowalsky / AFP via Getty Images) “Hey guys … Do you know where’s Abdul’s extended family in Alexandria specifically?” I woke up to that message in a WhatsApp group of reporters based in Cairo. It was the morning after the U.S. midterm elections and results were still coming in, but one race in particular was being followed by Egyptians thousands of miles away: Michigan’s U.S. Senate race. Abdul El-Sayed, the Egyptian-American doctor whose candidacy captured the attention of non-Egyptians too, had just delivered his victory speech. As his loyal supporters packed a ballroom in Detroit, in Cairo, the news was already making the rounds. My social media timelines were brimming with clips, memes and news items marking the occasion, and I started to imagine how the headlines here would tell the story of an “ibn el-balad” (“son of the land”) from Alexandria making his way into the American Senate. And although he is not the first Egyptian-American senator per se (that title belongs to George Helmy, who was appointed to represent New Jersey in 2024), he is the first one elected to the position — a distinction worth recognizing. Later that evening, at a gathering downtown that I’d tagged along to with a friend, I asked those around me — mostly filmmakers and writers, it seems — if they’d heard the news.   FULL TEXT: For The American Scholar , Lisa Russ Spaar offers us beautiful litany of loss in all its various shapes and forms. I’ve informally polled several friends, colleagues, and family members about their first remembered losses. For most, it was when a milk tooth fell out, usually around the age of six. She lost the names of things and people. She lost any memory of who we were, those of us who cared for her, even her devoted husband, my father. * The Poppy’s Many Meanings
  New Lines | 06 Nov 11:42 UTC
  The flower is used to commemorate sacrifice in war, but the humble weed has far more negative connotations in some countries. The post The Poppy’s Many Meanings appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: A planted seed is generally left well alone to sprout and grow in the darkness, perhaps with the help of water or fertilizer. But there is a notable exception: Poppies require light to germinate, meaning the earth must be disturbed before they grow. This feature of the flower explains much about its ubiquity and patterns across landscapes. It appears in particular density in places where earth has been turned over, such as farmland or cemeteries — or where trenches have been dug to protect troops in war. Nov. 11 is Remembrance Day across the Commonwealth, with poppies seen on lapels throughout the month, and memorial events held on the Sunday closest to the day itself — this year, this coming Sunday. These spots of color on coats symbolize remembrance of, and gratitude to, those who lost their lives fighting in war, drawing on images from World War I of the poppies that sprouted, undeterred by violence, all along the front lines of Europe. These front lines shifted regularly, often by tiny amounts, but each time requiring more trenches to be dug and more soil to be disrupted — so that more poppies grew. Poppies on battlefields had been noted during the Napoleonic Wars of the early 19th century, but the image was cemented in the minds of generations of schoolchildren by a poem scribbled in the back of an ambulance during World War I, by Canadian physician John McCrae. It begins: “In Flanders fields, the poppies blow / Between the crosses, row on row / That mark our place.” Its patriotism, signaling the rightness of dying for one’s country, is suitable for the military parades of Remembrance Sunday, and the simplicity and the color of the poppy make it an ideal emblem, adopted in 1921 by the newly formed Royal British Legion to fundraise to help those who served in the war. Neither the poem nor the poppy are uncontroversial, however. “Take up our quarrel with the foe!” the last verse begins, signaling a commitment to conflict many find abhorrent; these people shun the poppy or instead choose to wear a white version, symbolizing peace rather than war. But there are far wider problems, worldwide, with what poppies represent. From the opium wars in China to the poppy fields of India and Afghanistan to the opioid crisis in America, the poppy has been a source of addiction, conflict and misery — and, for a few, wealth. And these dichotomies, between wealth and grinding poverty, memories of noble sacrifice or of oppression and addiction, are not the only meanings 
  https://newlinesmag.com/essays/the-poppys-many-meanings/

* Celestial nuisance
  Aeon | 06 Nov 11:00 UTC
  The cosmos has become a lab for the Big Questions in physics. Along the way, we’ve lost sight of its glorious messiness - by Dylan Jow Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Journey to a satellite galaxy roughly 200,000 light-years from Earth, featuring the young star cluster NGC 602. With data from Webb’s NIRCam ( Near-InfraRed Camera ) and MIRI ( Mid-InfraRed Instrument ). Courtesy HUBBLE/WEBB/ESA In the story of cosmic history, one of the most important supporting characters is dust. As clouds of interstellar gas cooled and condensed in the early Universe, dust grains absorbed and re-emitted radiation, eventually forming stars. Later, dust provided the raw material from which planets could form and grow. Dust grains may even have been the surfaces on which the first complex organic molecules formed. Yet, if you ask most cosmologists, they’ll tell you that dust is a sheer nuisance. In astrophysics, ‘nuisance’ is a quasi-technical term. Astrophysicists call any phenomenon that gets in the way of the thing they actually want to study a ‘nuisance’. Dust grains emit polarised light that can mimic subtle signals from the afterglow of the Big Bang (the cosmic microwave background) that cosmologists have spent decades and billions of dollars trying to detect. A cosmologist studies dust not to uncover its role in shaping the cosmos, but to subtract it – the term ‘sweeping under the rug’ couldn’t be more apt. This dichotomy between ‘signal’ and ‘nuisance’ is found across many sub-fields in astronomy. Astronomers who study a phenomenon called ‘weak lensing’ use the subtle distortions of light caused by gravity to map invisible dark matter. For them, the ordinary matter that makes up everything we can see is a nuisance. Pulsar timing astronomers use rapidly spinning neutron stars called pulsars as extraordinarily precise clocks to detect the passage of gravitational waves through the galaxy. For them, the nuisance is ionised interstellar gas, which scatters and disperses the light from pulsars. Yet one astronomer’s nuisance is another’s treasure. Just as a laser beam reveals the intricate swirls of smoke in a dark room, pulsars illuminate the fine structure of the interstellar medium, the unlit space between galaxies. If you want to study the interstellar medium and its role in shaping our galaxy’s evolution, the light-scattering gas is an invaluable resource. The ESO-operated Very Large Telescope based at the Cerro Paranal site in the Atacama Desert of northern Chile. Photo by Miguel Claro/ESO Whether a phenomenon is a nuisance or something worth studying in its own right clearly depends on the investigator and their interests. But in
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-astrophysics-needs-to-abandon-the-big-questions

The fix O’Gorman endorses may sound drastic, but his precautionary principle is shared by invasion biology, the subdiscipline devoted to the study of species transplanted from their native ranges. There is a long history of organisms moved by humans, whether deliberately or inadvertently, wreaking total havoc upon their new homes. The brown tree snake, native to Indonesia, northern Australia, and Papua New Guinea, was accidentally introduced to Guam by the US military following World War II and promptly ate its way through ten of the island’s twelve endemic forest bird species. Similarly, Nile perch released into Lake Victoria by British colonial forces gulped down roughly two hundred species of homegrown cichlids, fish found nowhere else on Earth. Cane toads in Australia, Miconia trees on Hawaii and Tahiti, rats just about everywhere—the list goes on. And if biodiversity loss, however regrettable, seems like an abstraction in the scheme of things, know that human culture frequently suffers direct impoverishments because of invasives. Chestnut blight, discovered in the United States in 1904 after chestnut trees were imported from Japan, effectively deleted the American chestnut tree from the continent; its products were central to the diet and lifestyles of various indigenous peoples and communities across Appalachia. Phylloxera, an aphid relative, was imported to Europe on American grapevines during the second half of the nineteenth century. The subsequent infestation resulted in the destruction, by some estimates, of 90 percent of all European vineyards. “Godwits make the longest non-stop migration in the bird world. While Egypt clearly views Saudi security as intricately tied to regional stability, any military maneuver risks provoking a sustained retaliation. Egypt now believes that staying on the sidelines threatens its security more than asserting its might. The Houthis are already trying to change Egypt’s assessment. On Oct. 27, the Iran-backed group stated that they consider Cairo’s decision to aid Riyadh a “declaration of war.” The statement came a day after Egypt’s Parliament approved Sisi’s request to provide military support to Saudi Arabia if called upon to do so. Ahmed Aboudouh, an associate fellow of the Middle East and North Africa Program at Chatham House, believes that Egypt is preparing to proactively manage the risks. He told New Lines that the operations were likely to be defensive, but that this could mean not only defending from within Saudi territory, but also defending Egypt by attempting to limit the Houthis’ ability to attack Egyptian targets. “My sense is that Egypt’s role will be confined to air and naval defense,” Aboudouh added. He explained that Saudi Arabia’s vast size and critical assets make it difficult to intercept the barrage of Houthi airstrikes — a challenge that Egypt could help with. On Oct. 10, the Houthis hit Riyadh International Airport , killing 13 people and injuring more than 300. It was the deadliest attack on the kingdom in more than a decade. Ashley Stimpson | Longreads | November 5, 2026 | 18 minutes (4,985 words) In the months leading up to the nation’s bicentennial, the rickety wooden wheels of covered wagons began to roll in every corner of the United States—from the evergreen forests of Washington and windswept prairies of North Dakota to the granite peaks of Maine and palm tree-lined suburbs of Central Florida. Over the next year, the Bicentennial Wagon Train (BWT) would commemorate the founding of the country by performing the reverse of Westward Expansion, as five caravans—eventually made up of as many as 300 horse-drawn wagons—snaked their way toward Valley Forge, where they would arrive on July 4, 1976, just in time to celebrate America’s 200th birthday. Along the way, the wagon trains would stop in hundreds of cities and towns, where residents could watch teams of wagoneers construct historically accurate encampments and perform a musical revue. If so moved, citizens could also sign a so-called rededication scroll, to recommit themselves to building a more perfect union. “To commemorate this nation’s Bicentennial, we hereby dedicate ourselves anew to the precepts of our Founding Fathers,” each signer promised, inking their name on a sheath of scratchy parchment paper. Large community dinners and solemn ceremonies were planned around these vows to democracy; in some cities, whole elementary schools and entire churches lined up to sign. To accommodate those not on the wagons’ path, scroll-signing centers were set up in Holiday Inns from coast to coast. The scrolls were then loaded onto the Mayflower moving van that accompanied each train and carried toward Pennsylvania, where organizers planned to have them microfilmed and placed in an aluminum time capsule interred in the Pennsylvania dirt for the next 100 years. In 2076, organizers of the Bicentennial Wagon Train imagined, future Americans would unearth these scrolls and feel a deep sense of appreciation and admiration for the Americans of the past. Altogether, 22 million people (a tenth of the population at the time) signed approximately 917,000 scrolls, a stack of paper as high as a 20-story building . By all accounts these scrolls really did end up in Pennsylvania, where President Gerald Ford signed one, right before 
  https://longreads.com/2026/11/05/time-capsules-ashley-stimpson/

  FULL TEXT: On June 6, 1974, Jerome Wiesner booked into room 1537 at the Royal Tehran Hilton in preparation for his audience with the Shah of Iran. While his friends and family had mulled over what to gift him on his birthday, Wiesner was occupied by his own deliberations over what to gift the shah. “How about one of the crystals?” a colleague posited in a letter, referring to those grown aboard NASA’s first space station. “Two of them are said to be the most perfect crystals ever grown – because of the zero g environment, of course.” The MIT professors in possession of the perfect crystals might not be willing to part with an entire specimen, he admitted. “But I should think you might be able to persuade them to donate at least a millimeter slice.” Convinced, Wiesner arranged for a sliver of the space gem to be mounted on a velvet cushion. He was already in Tehran when an angry NASA official called MIT and mumbled about the upset at the Space Center. MIT was informed that “upper levels of NASA have ‘hit the ceiling.’” Turned out it wasn’t clear who actually owned the crystals, and Wiesner’s history as a thorn in NASA’s side didn’t help matters much. Mohammad Reza Pahlavi never received the perfect space crystal (gifted instead to President Gerald Ford a few months later), but Wiesner gave him something else at that meeting in Tehran that would end up being far more useful and far more polarizing. In 1975, MIT agreed to train two cohorts of 27 Iranian nuclear scientists in a three-year graduate program that would provide them with the skills necessary to run the reactors in Iran’s fledgling nuclear industry. The students were handpicked by the shah’s new atomic energy chief and had all expenses paid. At MIT, the special program caused an uproar that eventually receded into history, packed into numbered boxes in the university’s archives. * Believing and thinking

  The separation of church and state has an unspoken psychological basis: we choose what we believe, but not what we think - by Neil Van Leeuwen & Jacob Eisler Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Fryeburg, Maine, US. Photo by Andrew Lichtenstein/Corbis/Getty Images The idea of separating church and state – now entrenched in many modern liberal democracies – is in some ways strange. After all, governments must keep themselves informed and inform their citizens. That’s why they turn to the science of meteorology to predict weather patterns, to economics to understand patterns of growth and decline, and to the medical sciences to improve public health. So why wouldn’t governments also keep close alliances with religious institutions to know what’s going on with God? Separating church and state appears strange from other perspectives as well. In the historical sweep of large-scale human societies, it’s a relative newcomer, often associated with the United States’ founding father Thomas Jefferson. In 1802, Jefferson asserted the principle of separation in a letter to the Danbury Baptist Association in Connecticut: We’re fans of Jefferson’s principle, one of us (Neil Van Leeuwen) being a philosophy professor who grew up in the Christian Reformed Church, and the other (Jacob Eisler) being a law professor who regularly attends synagogue. But Jefferson’s letter makes putting the principle into practice sound simpler than it is – as though governments could be entirely hands-off when it comes to people’s beliefs, or ‘opinions’. That, however, can’t be right: engaging with and shaping political, social and other kinds of beliefs is actually a core function of government. Public education guides what people believe about everything from mathematics to history to astronomy. Governments also shape beliefs for policy reasons. If fire department officials learn that people believe a building is safe when it isn’t, they will try to change what people believe about the building’s safety. Belief is also a category of legal interest. If a psychotherapist, for example, believes a patient is a danger to himself or others, the therapist is obligated by law to report that belief. All this means that Jefferson’s ‘wall of separation’ can’t sensibly prohibit governments from intervening on beliefs of any type whatsoever. Rather, Jefferson – along with advocates of the principle who came after him – must have presupposed that there is something distinctive about religious beliefs that makes it reasonable to give them a different legal status. But what is this distinctive thing? Is it that religious beliefs concern God? No, because Buddhism is a religion with its own beliefs,
